I.2 Pareto Curves We illustrate the Pareto curves for the simultaneous PIs results with 19 target prediction levels in Section 6 , in terms of the coverage rate ( C ​ R CR ) (X-axis) and the interval width ( I ​ W IW ) (Y-axis), to offer a more intuitive comparison. The titles of all plots are named as the datasets in Section 6 . The arrangement of the plots are the same as the ones in Section I.1 . Specifically, each subplot contains two curves, one constructed with (input coverage, width) and another constructed with (achieved coverage, width). The curve representing the input coverage and width is shown in dashed line while the one representing the achieved coverage and width is shown in solid line along with a 90 % 90\% confidence interval as the shaded area. Each point in the line denotes the average obtained from N = 50 N=50 repetitions of trials. Ideally, a method performs well if in its Pareto curves, 1) the dashed line is on the left of the solid line, and 2) at the same time there is no level intersection between the solid line and the shaded area, meaning that within the same input coverage level, there is a sufficiently high probability in achieving the coverage level. Other than that, a smaller average I ​ W IW at each input coverage level is better since it refers to a less conservative predictive ability. From Figures 2 and 3 , we notice that CV+, NNGN, and NNGU have a lot more cases where there is no intersection between the dashed line and shaded area while SCL, QRF, SVMQR, SCQR and NNVA do not. Also the former three methods tend to generate much shorter PIs than the others. Specifically, there are 5 5 datasets where there is no intersection between the dashed line and the shaded area for CV+ and NNGN, and 4 4 for NNGU. However, NNGN and NNGU can achieve a much shorter M ​ I ​ W MIW (average of I ​ W IW over all input coverage levels) than the rest of the methods. One might notice that the two Pareto curves are a bit farther away from each other for NNVA, NNGN, and NNGU when the input coverage is small. This issue can be solved by choosing the calibration parameter more appropriately by, for example, training a more continuous spectrum of candidate models. Figure 2: Comparison of simultaneous PIs constructions in synthetic datasets 1 to 3, and Boston dataset. Dashed lines are constructed with (input coverage, width), and solid lines are constructed with (achieved coverage, width). Each point in the line denotes the average obtained from N = 50 N=50 repetitions of trials. Shaded area is the 90 % 90\% confidence interval. Figure 3: Comparison of simultaneous PIs constructions in Concrete, Energy, Wine and Yacht dataset. Dashed lines are constructed with (input coverage, width), and solid lines are constructed with (achieved coverage, width). Each point in the line denotes the average obtained from N = 50 N=50 repetitions of trials. Shaded area is the 90 % 90\% confidence interval. I.3 Implementation Details We elaborate more details about our experimental implementations in Sections 6 and I.1 . Datasets. Three synthetic datasets and five real-world benchmark datasets have been shown in Section 6 . The real-world datasets are the open-access datasets “Boston”, “Concrete”, “Energy”, “Wine” and “Yacht” that have been widely used in previous studies ( Hernández-Lobato and Adams 2015 ; Gal and Ghahramani 2016 ; Lakshminarayanan et al. 2017 ) for regression tasks. Table 4 shows their details. Dataset N d Open-access Link Boston: Boston Housing 506 13 kaggle.com/c/boston-housing Concrete: Concrete Strength 1030 8 kaggle.com/aakashphadtare/concrete-data Energy: Energy Efficiency 768 8 kaggle.com/elikplim/eergy-efficiency-dataset Wine: Red Wine Quality 1599 11 kaggle.com/uciml/red-wine-quality-cortez-et-al-2009 Yacht: Yacht Hydrodynamics 308 6 archive.ics.uci.edu/ml/datasets/yacht+hydrodynamics Table 4: Full names and details of benchmarking regression datasets. N N is the number of samples in the dataset and d d is the dimension of the feature vector. The data are split into training and testing sets as follows. For the methods where validation data are needed for calibration (NNVA, NNGN, NNGU), we use a proportion of training data as the validation data. In the single PI case, all of the real-world datasets have 80%/20% training/testing split. For NN methods, “Boston” has 24 % 24\% of data for validation; “Concrete” has 16 % 16\% of data for validation; “Energy” has 10 % 10\% of data for validation; “Wine” has 12 % 12\% of data for validation; “Yacht” has 15 % 15\% of data for validation. The three multivariate synthetic datasets in Section 6 have 1600/3000 training/testing split. For NN methods, 350 data points are used for validation. In the simultaneous PIs case, all the real-world datasets have 80%/20% training/testing split. For NN methods, “Boston” has 24 % 24\% of data for validation; “Concrete” has 16 % 16\% of data for validation; “Energy” has 24 % 24\% of data for validation; “Wine” has 24 % 24\% of data for validation; “Yacht” has 24 % 24\% of data for validation. The three multivariate synthetic datasets in Section 6 have 1600/3000 training/testing split. For NN methods, 350 data points are used for validation. In addition, for the experiments in Section I.1 , the three univariate synthetic datasets have 1200/300 training/testing split. For NN methods, 60 60 data points are used for validation. Implementations. We provide details of our algorithms and baseline approaches in Section 6 . They appear in the same order as in Tables 1 and 2 . (1) QRF: quantile regression forests, as proposed in Meinshausen 2006 . Our code is based on RandomForestQuantileRegressor from the package scikit-garden in Python. (2) CV+: CV+ prediction interval, as proposed in Section 3 in Barber et al. 2019 . In addition, the base regression algorithm is a neural network using mean square loss. (3) SCQR: split conformalized quantile regression, as proposed in Algorithm 1 in Romano et al. 2019 . The base quantile regression algorithm is exactly the QRF in (1). (4) SVMQR: quantile regression via support vector machine, the code of which is available in Steinwart and Thomann 2017 . (5) SCL: split conformal learning with correction. The original split conformal learning is described in Algorithm 2 in Lei et al. 2018 . The base regression algorithm is a neural network using mean square loss. Moreover, we apply a “correction” method to stipulate the coverage constraint with high confidence, which has been proposed in Equation 7 in Proposition 2b in Vovk 2012 to enhance the split/inductive conformal learning. Specifically, we change the prediction level 1 − α 1-\alpha to 1 − α ′ 1-\alpha^{\prime} by letting β ≥ b ​ i ​ n n v , α ​ ( ⌊ α ′ ​ ( n v + 1 ) − 1 ⌋ ) , \beta\geq bin_{n_{v},\alpha}(\lfloor\alpha^{\prime}(n_{v}+1)-1\rfloor), where 1 − β 1-\beta is the prefixed confidence level (90% throughout our experiments), n v n_{v} is the size of the calibration set and b ​ i ​ n n v , α bin_{n_{v},\alpha} is the cumulative binomial distribution function with n v n_{v} trials and probability of success α \alpha . (6) NNVA: neural networks using the loss shown in Section 6 with a vanilla scheme. In the vanilla scheme, we build multiple PI models by choosing different parameters λ &gt; 0 \lambda&gt;0 in the loss, and then select PIs with the smallest interval width among those whose empirical coverage rates on the validation dataset is larger than the target prediction levels, i.e., without the Gaussian margin in Algorithm 1 . (7) Ours-NNGN: neural networks using the loss shown in Section 6 with the normalized Gaussian PI calibration in Algorithm 1 . We build multiple PI models by choosing different parameters λ &gt; 0 \lambda&gt;0 in the loss. (8) Ours-NNGU: neural networks using the loss shown in Section 6 with the unnormalized Gaussian PI calibration in Algorithm 2 . We build multiple PI models by choosing different parameters λ &gt; 0 \lambda&gt;0 in the loss. For (6)(7)(8), in order to improve the training of the neural networks, we use the ensemble technique in Pearce et al. 2018 . That is, instead of directly taking the outputs of one network, we train networks several times by using different initializations and then define the final prediction U U and L L as U = U ¯ + 1.96 ​ σ U , where ​ U ¯ = 1 e ​ ∑ j = 1 e U ^ j , σ U = 1 e − 1 ​ ∑ j = 1 e ( U ^ j − U ¯ ) 2 \displaystyle U=\overline{U}+1.96\sigma_{U},\text{ where }\overline{U}=\frac{1}{e}\sum_{j=1}^{e}\hat{U}_{j},\ \sigma_{U}=\frac{1}{e-1}\sum_{j=1}^{e}(\hat{U}_{j}-\overline{U})^{2} L = L ¯ − 1.96 ​ σ L , where ​ L ¯ = 1 e ​ ∑ j = 1 e L ^ j , σ L = 1 e − 1 ​ ∑ j = 1 e ( L ^ j − L ¯ ) 2 \displaystyle{L}=\overline{L}-1.96\sigma_{L},\text{ where }\overline{L}=\frac{1}{e}\sum_{j=1}^{e}\hat{L}_{j},\ \sigma_{L}=\frac{1}{e-1}\sum_{j=1}^{e}(\hat{L}_{j}-\overline{L})^{2} where e e denotes the ensemble size. Architectures and Hyper-parameters. For (2)(5)(6)(7)(8), we train fully connected neural networks with ReLU activations, using the Adam method for stochastic optimization. Note that the base neural networks in (2)(5) have only one output unit (point prediction) while our neural networks in (6)(7)(8) have two output units (lower and upper bounds of PIs). Nevertheless, the neural networks in (2)(5)(6)(7)(8) have the same architecture of hidden layers on each dataset. On “Boston” and “Concrete”, we have 1 hidden layer and each hidden layer has 50 50 neurons. On “Energy”, “Wine” and “Yacht”, we have 2 hidden layers and each hidden layer has 64 64 neurons. On three multivariate synthetic datasets in Section 6 , we have 2 hidden layers and each hidden layer has 50 50 neurons. On the three univariate synthetic datasets in Section I.1 , we have 1 hidden layer and each hidden layer has 50 50 neurons. In addition, N = 50 N=50 repetitions of trials are run on each dataset. The confidence level is 1 − β = 90 % 1-\beta=90\% in all experiments. Appendix J Technical Proofs J.1 Proofs for Results in Section 4 Proof of Theorem 4.1 . We first present a lemma: Lemma J.1 . Let ‖ g ‖ Q , 2 \|g\|_{Q,2} be the L 2 L_{2} -norm of a function g g under a probability measure Q Q . Lemma J.3 (Adapted from Theorem 2.14.1 in Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996 ) . Using the notations from Lemma J.2 , we define J ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) := sup Q ∫ 0 1 1 + log ⁡ N ⁡ ( ϵ ​ ‖ G ‖ Q , 2 , 𝒢 , L 2 ​ ( Q ) ) ​ 𝑑 ϵ J(\mathcal{G}):=\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon\|G\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{G},L_{2}(Q))}d\epsilon where the supremum is taken over all discrete probability measures Q Q with ‖ G ‖ Q , 2 &lt; ∞ \|G\|_{Q,2}&lt;\infty . As a side note, a rigorous statement for the results in Lemma J.3 involves a so-called P-measurability condition for the class 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , but we choose not to deal with the measurability requirement here. P-measurability holds for common function classes, e.g., if there exists a countable subclass 𝒢 ′ ⊂ 𝒢 \mathcal{G}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{G} such that for every g ∈ 𝒢 g\in\mathcal{G} there exists a sequence from 𝒢 ′ \mathcal{G}^{\prime} that converges to g g point-wise. We need one more result: Lemma J.4 (Adapted from Theorem 2.14.5 in Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996 ) . Using the notations from Lemma J.2 , we have ‖ sup g ∈ 𝒢 | 𝔼 π ^ X ​ [ g ⁡ ( X ) ] − 𝔼 π X ​ [ g ⁡ ( X ) ] | ‖ ψ 2 \displaystyle\|\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\pi}_{X}}[g(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[g(X)]\rvert\|_{\psi_{2}} ≤ \displaystyle\leq C ⁡ ( ‖ sup g ∈ 𝒢 | 𝔼 π ^ X ​ [ g ⁡ ( X ) ] − 𝔼 π X ​ [ g ⁡ ( X ) ] | ‖ 1 + 1 n ⋅ ‖ G ‖ ψ 2 ) \displaystyle C\big(\|\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\pi}_{X}}[g(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[g(X)]\rvert\|_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\|G\|_{\psi_{2}}\big) where the sub-Gaussian norm ∥ ⋅ ∥ ψ 2 \|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}} on the left hand size is with respect to the product measure π X n \pi_{X}^{n} (i.e., the data), and C C is a universal constant. We now turn to the main proof. We first deal with ϕ 1 \phi_{1} . Applying the bound in Lemma J.3 to the class ℋ c \mathcal{H}_{c} gives ‖ sup h ∈ ℋ c | 𝔼 π ^ X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] − 𝔼 π X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] | ‖ 1 ≤ vc ⁡ ( ℋ + ) n ⋅ C ​ ‖ H ‖ 2 . \|\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{c}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\pi}_{X}}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[h(X)]\rvert\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{H}_{+}\right)}{n}}\cdot C\|H\|_{2}. The sub-Gaussian tail bound then gives the expression for ϕ 1 \phi_{1} . Next we analyze ϕ 2 \phi_{2} . First note that ℋ ⊂ ℋ + \mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{H}_{+} , therefore vc ⁡ ( ℋ ) ≤ vc ⁡ ( ℋ + ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)\leq\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{H}_{+}\right) . By the definition of VC-subgraph, both its closed subgraph class 𝒮 u ​ p ​ p ​ e ​ r := { { ( x , y ) : y ≤ U ⁡ ( x ) } : U ∈ ℋ } \mathcal{S}_{upper}:=\{\{(x,y):y\leq U(x)\}:U\in\mathcal{H}\} and open subgraph class 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ′ := { { ( x , y ) : y &lt; L ⁡ ( x ) } : L ∈ ℋ } \mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{lower}:=\{\{(x,y):y&lt;L(x)\}:L\in\mathcal{H}\} have a VC dimension vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 u ​ p ​ p ​ e ​ r ) = vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ′ ) = vc ⁡ ( ℋ ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{upper}\right)=\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{lower}\right)=\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{H}\right) (using ≤ \leq or &lt; &lt; for defining subgraphs does not affect the resulting VC dimension, see Problem 10 from Section 2.6 in Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996 ). To proceed, we need the following preservation result for VC classes: Lemma J.5 (Adapted from Lemma 9.7 statements (i) and (v) in Kosorok 2007 ) . Let 𝒮 \mathcal{S} be a VC class of sets in a space 𝕊 \mathbb{S} , then 1. ψ : 𝕊 → 𝕊 \psi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{S} be a one-to-one mapping, then the class ψ ⁡ ( 𝒮 ) := { { ψ ⁡ ( s ) : s ∈ S } : S ∈ 𝒮 } \psi(\mathcal{S}):=\{\{\psi(s):s\in S\}:S\in\mathcal{S}\} is also a VC class with vc ⁡ ( ψ ⁡ ( 𝒮 ) ) = vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\psi(\mathcal{S})\right)=\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}\right) 2. The complement class 𝒮 c := { 𝕊 \ S : S ∈ 𝒮 } \mathcal{S}^{c}:=\{\mathbb{S}\backslash S:S\in\mathcal{S}\} is a VC class with vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 c ) = vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}^{c}\right)=\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}\right) . and a VC dimension bound for unions and intersections of VC classes: Lemma J.6 (Adapted from Theorem 1.1 in Van Der Vaart and Wellner 2009 ) . Suppose 𝒮 1 , … , 𝒮 K \mathcal{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{K} are VC classes of sets in a space 𝕊 \mathbb{S} . Define ⊔ k = 1 K 𝒮 k := { ∪ k = 1 K S k : S k ∈ 𝒮 k for k = 1 , … , K } \sqcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{S}_{k}:=\{\cup_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}:S_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,K\} and ⊓ k = 1 K 𝒮 k := { ∩ k = 1 K S k : S k ∈ 𝒮 k for k = 1 , … , K } \sqcap_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{S}_{k}:=\{\cap_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}:S_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,K\} . We have vc ( ⊔ k = 1 K 𝒮 k ) ≤ C log ( K ) ∑ k = 1 K vc ( 𝒮 k ) , vc ( ⊓ k = 1 K 𝒮 k ) ≤ C log ( K ) ∑ k = 1 K vc ( 𝒮 k ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\sqcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{S}_{k}\right)\leq C\log(K)\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{k}\right),\;\;\;\mathrm{vc}\left(\sqcap_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{S}_{k}\right)\leq C\log(K)\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{k}\right) for some universal constant C C . Further consider 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r := { { ( x , y ) : y ≥ L ⁡ ( x ) } : L ∈ ℋ } \mathcal{S}_{lower}:=\{\{(x,y):y\geq L(x)\}:L\in\mathcal{H}\} , and 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w := { { ( x , y ) : L ( x ) ≤ y ≤ U ( x ) } : L , U ∈ ℋ and L ≤ U } \mathcal{S}_{btw}:=\{\{(x,y):L(x)\leq y\leq U(x)\}:L,U\in\mathcal{H}\text{ and }L\leq U\} . We observe that 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r = 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ′ c \mathcal{S}_{lower}=\mathcal{S}_{lower}^{\prime c} , and that 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ⊂ 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ⊓ 𝒮 u ​ p ​ p ​ e ​ r \mathcal{S}_{btw}\subset\mathcal{S}_{lower}\sqcap\mathcal{S}_{upper} . Therefore by Lemma J.5 we have vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ) = vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ′ ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{lower}\right)=\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{lower}\right) , and applying the bound for intersection from Lemma J.6 to 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w \mathcal{S}_{btw} gives vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ) ≤ vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 l ​ o ​ w ​ e ​ r ⊓ 𝒮 u ​ p ​ p ​ e ​ r ) ≤ C ​ vc ​ ( 𝒮 u ​ p ​ p ​ e ​ r ) = C ​ vc ​ ( ℋ ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{btw}\right)\leq\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{lower}\sqcap\mathcal{S}_{upper}\right)\leq C\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{upper}\right)=C\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{H}\right) for some universal constant C C . With this VC bound for 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w \mathcal{S}_{btw} , we are ready to use standard deviation bounds for VC set classes (see, e.g., equation (3.3) in Vapnik 2013 ) to get sup L , U ∈ ℋ ​ and ​ L ≤ U ℙ ⁡ ( | ℙ π ^ ​ ( L ⁡ ( X ) ≤ Y ≤ U ⁡ ( X ) ) − ℙ π ​ ( L ⁡ ( X ) ≤ Y ≤ U ⁡ ( X ) ) | &gt; t ) \displaystyle\sup_{L,U\in\mathcal{H}\;\text{and}\;L\leq U}\mathbb{P}(\lvert\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\pi}}(L(X)\leq Y\leq U(X))-\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(L(X)\leq Y\leq U(X))\rvert&gt;t) = \displaystyle= sup S ∈ 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ℙ ⁡ ( | ℙ π ^ ​ ( ( X , Y ) ∈ S ) − ℙ π ​ ( ( X , Y ) ∈ S ) | &gt; t ) \displaystyle\sup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{btw}}\mathbb{P}(\lvert\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\pi}}((X,Y)\in S)-\mathbb{P}_{\pi}((X,Y)\in S)\rvert&gt;t) ≤ \displaystyle\leq 4 ​ G ​ r ​ o ​ w ​ t ​ h ​ ( 2 ​ n ) ​ exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle 4\mathrm{Growth}(2n)\exp(-t^{2}n) where Growth ⁡ ( 2 ​ n ) \mathrm{Growth}(2n) is the growth function, or the shattering number, for the class 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w \mathcal{S}_{btw} . By the Sauer–Shelah lemma we have Growth ⁡ ( 2 ​ n ) = 2 2 ​ n \mathrm{Growth}(2n)=2^{2n} if 2 ​ n &lt; vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ) 2n&lt;\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{btw}\right) and ≤ ( 2 ​ e ​ n vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ) ) vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ) \leq\big(\frac{2en}{\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{btw}\right)}\big)^{\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{btw}\right)} if 2 ​ n ≥ vc ⁡ ( 𝒮 b ​ t ​ w ) 2n\geq\mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{S}_{btw}\right) . The feasibility and optimality bound for δ ^ t ∗ \hat{\delta}_{t}^{*} can be obtained by solving ϕ 1 ​ ( n , ϵ , ℋ ) = η 2 \phi_{1}(n,\epsilon,\mathcal{H})=\frac{\eta}{2} and ϕ 2 ​ ( n , t , ℋ ) = η 2 \phi_{2}(n,t,\mathcal{H})=\frac{\eta}{2} for ϵ \epsilon and t t , and then applying Theorem 4.2 . ∎ Proof of Theorem 4.4 . We first treat ϕ 1 \phi_{1} . We need a maximal inequality that is similar to Lemma J.3 , but based on bracketing numbers instead: Lemma J.7 (Adapted from Theorem 2.14.2 in Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996 ) . Proof of Lemma J.8 . By a change of variable t = log ⁡ 1 ϵ t=\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}} , we write ∫ 0 c log ⁡ 1 ϵ ​ 𝑑 ϵ \displaystyle\int_{0}^{c}\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}d\epsilon = \displaystyle= ∫ log ⁡ 1 c ∞ 2 ​ t 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}}^{\infty}2t^{2}\exp(-t^{2})dt = \displaystyle= − t ​ exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ) | t = log ⁡ 1 c t = ∞ + ∫ log ⁡ 1 c ∞ exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle-t\exp(-t^{2})\Big|_{t=\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}}^{t=\infty}+\int_{\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}}^{\infty}\exp(-t^{2})dt ≤ \displaystyle\leq c ​ log ⁡ 1 c + ∫ log ⁡ 1 c ∞ t log ⁡ 1 c ​ exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle c\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}+\int_{\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}}^{\infty}\frac{t}{\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}}\exp(-t^{2})dt = \displaystyle= c ​ log ⁡ 1 c + c 2 ​ log ⁡ 1 c ≤ ( 1 + 1 2 ​ log ⁡ 3 ) ​ c ​ log ⁡ 1 c \displaystyle c\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}+\frac{c}{2\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}}}\leq\big(1+\frac{1}{2\log 3}\big)c\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{c}} where in the last line we use c ≤ 1 3 c\leq\frac{1}{3} . ∎ Lemma J.7 then entails the following maximal inequality for the centered class ℋ c \mathcal{H}_{c} ‖ sup h ∈ ℋ c | 𝔼 π ^ X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] − 𝔼 π X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] | ‖ 1 ≤ l n ⋅ C ​ max ⁡ { log ⁡ diam ⁡ ( Θ ) ​ ‖ ℒ ‖ 2 ‖ H ‖ 2 , 1 } ​ ‖ H ‖ 2 . \|\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{c}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\pi}_{X}}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[h(X)]\rvert\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\frac{l}{n}}\cdot C\sqrt{\max\{\log\frac{\mathrm{diam}\left(\Theta\right)\|\mathcal{L}\|_{2}}{\|H\|_{2}},1\}}\|H\|_{2}. (J.2) To derive the deviation bound using the bracketing number ( J.2 ), we need one more result: Lemma J.9 (Adapted from Theorem 6.8 in Talagrand 1994 ) . Suppose 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is a class of measurable indicator functions from 𝒳 × ℝ → ℝ \mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} , and that its ϵ \epsilon -bracketing number N [ ] ​ ( ϵ , 𝒢 , L 1 ​ ( π ) ) ≤ ( V ϵ ) ν N_{[]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{G},L_{1}(\pi))\leq\big(\frac{V}{\epsilon}\big)^{\nu} for V , ν &gt; 0 V,\nu&gt;0 , then there exists a universal constant C C such that for all t ≥ C ​ ν ​ log ⁡ ( V ) ​ log ⁡ log ​ ( V ) n t\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\nu\log(V)\log\log(V)}{n}} we have ℙ ⁡ ( sup g ∈ 𝒢 | 𝔼 π ^ ​ [ g ⁡ ( X , Y ) ] − 𝔼 π ​ [ g ⁡ ( X , Y ) ] | &gt; t ) ≤ C t ​ n ​ ( C ​ V ​ t 2 ​ n ν ) ν ​ exp ⁡ ( − 2 ​ t 2 ​ n ) . \mathbb{P}(\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\pi}}[g(X,Y)]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[g(X,Y)]\rvert&gt;t)\leq\frac{C}{t\sqrt{n}}\big(\frac{CVt^{2}n}{\nu}\big)^{\nu}\exp(-2t^{2}n). To make the bound ( J.3 ) valid for small t t , we next enlarge the constant C C so that the bound ( J.3 ) is trivial. Replacing C 2 C^{2} with C C in the above bound gives the expression for ϕ 2 \phi_{2} . Finally, like in Theorem 4.3 , we solve ϕ 1 ​ ( n , ϵ , ℋ ) = η 2 \phi_{1}(n,\epsilon,\mathcal{H})=\frac{\eta}{2} and ϕ 2 ​ ( n , t , ℋ ) = η 2 \phi_{2}(n,t,\mathcal{H})=\frac{\eta}{2} for ϵ \epsilon and t t , and then apply Theorem 4.2 to get the feasibility and optimality errors. We briefly explain how t t is derived. Consider the case t 2 ​ n 2 ​ C ​ l ≥ 1 \frac{t^{2}n}{2Cl}\geq 1 , so we can derive the following upper bound for ϕ 2 \phi_{2} ϕ 2 ​ ( n , t , ℋ ) \displaystyle\phi_{2}(n,t,\mathcal{H}) ≤ \displaystyle\leq ( C ℋ 2 ​ C ​ log ⁡ log ​ ( C ℋ ) ​ t 2 ​ n 2 ​ C ​ l ) 2 ​ l ​ exp ⁡ ( − 2 ​ t 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle\Big(C_{\mathcal{H}}^{2C\log\log(C_{\mathcal{H}})}\frac{t^{2}n}{2Cl}\Big)^{2l}\exp(-2t^{2}n) = \displaystyle= ( C ℋ 2 ​ C ​ log ⁡ log ​ ( C ℋ ) C ) 2 ​ l ⋅ ( t 2 ​ n 2 ​ l ​ exp ⁡ ( − 2 ​ t 2 ​ n 2 ​ l ) ) 2 ​ l \displaystyle\Big(\frac{C_{\mathcal{H}}^{2C\log\log(C_{\mathcal{H}})}}{C}\Big)^{2l}\cdot\big(\frac{t^{2}n}{2l}\exp(-2\frac{t^{2}n}{2l})\big)^{2l} &lt; \displaystyle&lt; ( C ℋ 2 ​ C ​ log ⁡ log ​ ( C ℋ ) C ) 2 ​ l ⋅ ( exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ​ n 2 ​ l ) ) 2 ​ l ​ using ​ t 2 ​ n 2 ​ l &lt; exp ⁡ ( t 2 ​ n 2 ​ l ) \displaystyle\Big(\frac{C_{\mathcal{H}}^{2C\log\log(C_{\mathcal{H}})}}{C}\Big)^{2l}\cdot\big(\exp(-\frac{t^{2}n}{2l})\big)^{2l}\text{\ \ \ using }\frac{t^{2}n}{2l}&lt;\exp(\frac{t^{2}n}{2l}) ≤ \displaystyle\leq ( C ℋ 2 ​ C ​ log ⁡ log ​ ( C ℋ ) ) 2 ​ l ⋅ exp ⁡ ( − t 2 ​ n ) ​ assuming ​ C ≥ 1 . \displaystyle\Big(C_{\mathcal{H}}^{2C\log\log(C_{\mathcal{H}})}\Big)^{2l}\cdot\exp(-t^{2}n)\text{\ \ \ \ assuming }C\geq 1. The choice of t t presented in the theorem is obtained by equating this upper bound to η 2 \frac{\eta}{2} . ∎ Proof of Theorem 4.5 . The regression tree class ℋ \mathcal{H} consists of all functions that of form h ⁡ ( x ) = ∑ s = 1 S + 1 c s ​ I x ∈ R s h(x)=\sum_{s=1}^{S+1}c_{s}I_{x\in R_{s}} , and note that the augmented class ℋ + \mathcal{H}_{+} consists of functions ∑ s = 1 S + 1 ( c s − c ) ​ I x ∈ R s \sum_{s=1}^{S+1}(c_{s}-c)I_{x\in R_{s}} which are of the same form, therefore ℋ = ℋ + \mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{+} . As discussed before, the subgraph of a regression tree takes the form of the union of at most S + 1 S+1 hyper-rectangles in ℝ d + 1 \mathbb{R}^{d+1} , where each rectangle is formed by at most S S axis-parallel cuts. We first calculate the VC dimension of the set of all axis-parallel cuts. Four sets of axis-parallel cuts in ℝ d + 1 \mathbb{R}^{d+1} are defined as 𝒞 d + 1 ≤ \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\leq}_{d+1} : = { { ( x 1 , … , x d + 1 ) ∈ ℝ d + 1 : x j ≤ a } : j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , d + 1 } , a ∈ [ − ∞ , + ∞ ] } \displaystyle:=\{\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:x_{j}\leq a\}:j\in\{1,2,\ldots,d+1\},a\in[-\infty,+\infty]\} 𝒞 d + 1 &lt; \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{&lt;}_{d+1} : = { { ( x 1 , … , x d + 1 ) ∈ ℝ d + 1 : x j &lt; a } : j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , d + 1 } , a ∈ [ − ∞ , + ∞ ] } \displaystyle:=\{\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:x_{j}&lt;a\}:j\in\{1,2,\ldots,d+1\},a\in[-\infty,+\infty]\} 𝒞 d + 1 ≥ \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\geq}_{d+1} : = { { ( x 1 , … , x d + 1 ) ∈ ℝ d + 1 : x j ≥ a } : j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , d + 1 } , a ∈ [ − ∞ , + ∞ ] } \displaystyle:=\{\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:x_{j}\geq a\}:j\in\{1,2,\ldots,d+1\},a\in[-\infty,+\infty]\} 𝒞 d + 1 &gt; \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{&gt;}_{d+1} : = { { ( x 1 , … , x d + 1 ) ∈ ℝ d + 1 : x j &gt; a } : j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , d + 1 } , a ∈ [ − ∞ , + ∞ ] } . \displaystyle:=\{\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:x_{j}&gt;a\}:j\in\{1,2,\ldots,d+1\},a\in[-\infty,+\infty]\}. Proposition 1 in Gey 2018 states that vc ⁡ ( 𝒞 d + 1 ≤ ) ≤ C ​ log ⁡ d \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\leq}_{d+1}\right)\leq C\log d for some universal constant C C . Note that 𝒞 d + 1 &gt; \mathcal{C}^{&gt;}_{d+1} is the complement class of 𝒞 d + 1 ≤ \mathcal{C}^{\leq}_{d+1} , therefore by Lemma J.5 statement 2 we have the same bound for vc ⁡ ( 𝒞 d + 1 &gt; ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{C}^{&gt;}_{d+1}\right) . The class 𝒞 d + 1 ≥ \mathcal{C}^{\geq}_{d+1} (resp. 𝒞 d + 1 &lt; \mathcal{C}^{&lt;}_{d+1} ) can be mapped from 𝒞 d + 1 ≤ \mathcal{C}^{\leq}_{d+1} (resp. 𝒞 d + 1 &gt; \mathcal{C}^{&gt;}_{d+1} ) via the mapping ( x 1 , … , x d + 1 ) → ( − x 1 , … , − x d + 1 ) (x_{1},\ldots,x_{d+1})\to(-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{d+1}) , therefore by statement 1 in Lemma J.5 we have the same VC bound for them too. Using Lemma J.6 we know that the VC bound for the set of all axis-parallel cuts 𝒞 d + 1 := 𝒞 d + 1 ≤ ⊔ 𝒞 d + 1 &lt; ⊔ 𝒞 d + 1 ≥ ⊔ 𝒞 d + 1 &gt; \mathcal{C}_{d+1}:=\mathcal{C}^{\leq}_{d+1}\sqcup\mathcal{C}^{&lt;}_{d+1}\sqcup\mathcal{C}^{\geq}_{d+1}\sqcup\mathcal{C}^{&gt;}_{d+1} has VC dimension vc ⁡ ( 𝒞 d + 1 ) ≤ C ​ log ⁡ d \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{C}_{d+1}\right)\leq C\log d . Now we can readily obtain vc ⁡ ( ℋ ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{H}\right) via intersections and unions. Each hyper-rectangle R s × ( − ∞ , c s ) R_{s}\times(-\infty,c_{s}) is the intersection of at most S + 1 S+1 axis-parallel cuts in ℝ d + 1 \mathbb{R}^{d+1} . Formally, denoting by ℛ \mathcal{R} as set of all such hyper-rectangles, then ℛ ⊂ ⊓ s = 1 S + 1 𝒞 d + 1 \mathcal{R}\subset\sqcap_{s=1}^{S+1}\mathcal{C}_{d+1} , and hence vc ⁡ ( ℛ ) ≤ C ​ S ​ log ⁡ ( d ) ​ log ⁡ ( S ) \mathrm{vc}\left(\mathcal{R}\right)\leq CS\log(d)\log(S) by Lemma J.6 . Moreover, the set of subgraphs of regression trees is a subset of ⊔ s = 1 S + 1 ℛ \sqcup_{s=1}^{S+1}\mathcal{R} , therefore the VC dimension of the set of subgraphs is at most C ​ S 2 ​ log ⁡ ( d ) ​ log 2 ⁡ ( S ) CS^{2}\log(d)\log^{2}(S) again by Lemma J.6 . Finally, when max x ⁡ h ⁡ ( x ) − min x ⁡ h ⁡ ( x ) ≤ M \max_{x}h(x)-\min_{x}h(x)\leq M , then the envelope H ≤ 2 ​ M H\leq 2M in Theorem 4.3 , therefore the sub-Gaussian norm ‖ H ‖ ψ 2 ≤ C ′ ​ M \|H\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C^{\prime}M for some universal constant C ′ C^{\prime} . ∎ Proof of Theorem 4.6 . To make the parameterizaton more instructive, we explicitly write θ = ( W 1 , b 1 , … , W S , b S ) \theta=(W_{1},b_{1},\ldots,W_{S},b_{S}) in terms of the weights and biases. We consider a perturbation Δ ​ θ := ( Δ ​ W 1 , Δ ​ b 1 , … , Δ ​ W S , Δ ​ b S ) \Delta\theta:=(\Delta W_{1},\Delta b_{1},\ldots,\Delta W_{S},\Delta b_{S}) , and wants to bound the difference | h ⁡ ( x , θ + Δ ​ θ ) − h ⁡ ( x , θ ) | \lvert h(x,\theta+\Delta\theta)-h(x,\theta)\rvert . Denoting W s ′ = W s + Δ ​ W s W^{\prime}_{s}=W_{s}+\Delta W_{s} and b s ′ = b s + Δ ​ b s b^{\prime}_{s}=b_{s}+\Delta b_{s} , we define two mappings ψ s : S \displaystyle\psi_{s:S} : x ∈ ℝ n s → ϕ S ( W S ϕ S − 1 ( ⋯ ϕ s + 1 ( W s + 1 ϕ s ( x ) + b s + 1 ) ⋯ ) + b S ) ∈ ℝ \displaystyle:x\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\to\phi_{S}(W_{S}\phi_{S-1}(\cdots\phi_{s+1}(W_{s+1}\phi_{s}(x)+b_{s+1})\cdots)+b_{S})\in\mathbb{R} ψ ′ 0 : s − 1 \displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{0:s-1} : x ∈ 𝒳 → ϕ s − 1 ( W s − 1 ′ ϕ s − 2 ( ⋯ ϕ 1 ( W 1 ′ x + b 1 ′ ) ⋯ ) + b s − 1 ′ ) ∈ ℝ n s − 1 . \displaystyle:x\in\mathcal{X}\to\phi_{s-1}(W^{\prime}_{s-1}\phi_{s-2}(\cdots\phi_{1}(W^{\prime}_{1}x+b^{\prime}_{1})\cdots)+b^{\prime}_{s-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s-1}}. Therefore the problem boils down to bounding the Lipschitz constant L ψ s : S L_{\psi_{s:S}} and the norm of the intermediate output ∥ ψ 0 : s − 1 ′ ( x ) ∥ 2 \|\psi^{\prime}_{0:s-1}(x)\|_{2} . Let { X i = ( X i ( 1 ) , … , X i ( m ) ) : i = 1 , … , n \{X_{i}=(X_{i}^{(1)},\ldots,X_{i}^{(m)}):i=1,\ldots,n } be an i.i.d. data set from ℝ m \mathbb{R}^{m} . Let X ¯ ( j ) = ( 1 / n ) ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i ( j ) \bar{X}^{(j)}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{(j)} be the sample mean of the j j -th component, and Σ ^ \hat{\Sigma} be the sample covariance matrix formed from the data. We denote by Z ^ := ( Z ^ ( 1 ) , … , Z ^ ( m ) ) \hat{Z}:=(\hat{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{(m)}) an m m -dimensional multivariate Gaussian with mean zero and covariance Σ ^ \hat{\Sigma} . Then under several light-tail conditions: Assumption 3 . Var ⁡ [ X 1 ( j ) ] &gt; 0 \mathrm{Var}[X_{1}^{(j)}]&gt;0 for all j = 1 , … , m j=1,\ldots,m and there exists some constant D ≥ 1 D\geq 1 such that 𝔼 ⁡ [ exp ⁡ ( | X 1 ( j ) − 𝔼 [ X 1 ( j ) ] | 2 D 2 ​ Var ​ [ X 1 ( j ) ] ) ] ≤ 2 ​ for all ​ j = 1 , … , m \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\exp\Big(\frac{|X_{1}^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{(j)}]\rvert^{2}}{D^{2}\mathrm{Var}[X_{1}^{(j)}]}\Big)\big]\leq 2\text{ for all }j=1,\ldots,m 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( | X 1 ( j ) − 𝔼 ⁡ [ X 1 ( j ) ] | Var ⁡ [ X 1 ( j ) ] ) 2 + k ] ≤ D k ​ for all ​ j = 1 , … , m ​ and ​ k = 1 , 2 . \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\frac{\lvert X_{1}^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{(j)}]\rvert}{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[X_{1}^{(j)}]}}\Big)^{2+k}\big]\leq D^{k}\text{ for all }j=1,\ldots,m\text{ and }k=1,2. Assumption 4 . Each X 1 ( j ) X_{1}^{(j)} is [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] -valued and Var ⁡ [ X 1 ( j ) ] ≥ η \mathrm{Var}[X_{1}^{(j)}]\geq\eta for all j = 1 , … , m j=1,\ldots,m and some constant η &gt; 0 \eta&gt;0 . we have the following Berry Esseen theorems ( Chernozhukov et al. 2017 ): Lemma J.10 (Unnormalized supremum, adopted from Theorem EC.9 in Lam and Qian 2019 ) . It suffices to show that the induced set class is strong π \pi -GC. Consistency then follows from Theorem A.1 and the fact that strong GC implies weak GC. We will need the following preservation results for GC classes: Lemma J.12 (Adapted from Theorem 9.26 in Kosorok 2007 ) . Suppose that 𝒢 1 , … , 𝒢 K \mathcal{G}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{K} are strong π \pi -GC classes of functions with max ⁡ sup g ∈ 𝒢 k 1 ≤ k ≤ K ⁡ | 𝔼 π ​ [ g ⁡ ( X , Y ) ] | &lt; ∞ \max_{1\leq k\leq K}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[g(X,Y)]\rvert&lt;\infty , and that ψ : ℝ K → ℝ \psi:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R} is continuous. Then the class ψ ( 𝒢 1 , … , 𝒢 K ) := { ψ ( g 1 ( ⋅ ) , … , g K ( ⋅ ) ) : g k ∈ 𝒢 k for k = 1 , … , K } \psi(\mathcal{G}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{K}):=\{\psi(g_{1}(\cdot),\ldots,g_{K}(\cdot)):g_{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{k}\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,K\} is strong π \pi -GC provided that 𝔼 π ​ [ sup g ∈ ψ ⁡ ( 𝒢 1 , … , 𝒢 K ) | g ⁡ ( X , Y ) | ] &lt; ∞ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\sup_{g\in\psi(\mathcal{G}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{K})}\lvert g(X,Y)\rvert]&lt;\infty . We first use Lemma J.12 to simplify the problem. Denote by 𝒢 := { ( x , y ) → I L ⁡ ( x ) ≤ y ≤ U ⁡ ( x ) : L , U ∈ ℋ , L ≤ U } \mathcal{G}:=\{(x,y)\to I_{L(x)\leq y\leq U(x)}:L,U\in\mathcal{H},L\leq U\} the target indicator class for which we want to show strong π \pi -GC. Then 𝒢 ⊂ ψ ⁡ ( 𝒢 l , 𝒢 u ) \mathcal{G}\subset\psi(\mathcal{G}_{l},\mathcal{G}_{u}) , where ψ ⁡ ( z 1 , z 2 ) := z 1 ​ z 2 \psi(z_{1},z_{2}):=z_{1}z_{2} , and 𝒢 l \displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{l} : = { ( x , y ) → I L ⁡ ( x ) − y ≤ 0 : L ∈ ℋ } \displaystyle:=\{(x,y)\to I_{L(x)-y\leq 0}:L\in\mathcal{H}\} 𝒢 u \displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{u} : = { ( x , y ) → I y − U ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 0 : U ∈ ℋ } . \displaystyle:=\{(x,y)\to I_{y-U(x)\leq 0}:U\in\mathcal{H}\}. Note that functions in the class ψ ⁡ ( 𝒢 l , 𝒢 u ) \psi(\mathcal{G}_{l},\mathcal{G}_{u}) are all bounded by 1 1 and ψ ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \psi(\cdot,\cdot) is obviously continuous, therefore by Lemma J.12 , ψ ⁡ ( 𝒢 l , 𝒢 u ) \psi(\mathcal{G}_{l},\mathcal{G}_{u}) (hence 𝒢 \mathcal{G} ) is strong π \pi -GC if both 𝒢 l \mathcal{G}_{l} and 𝒢 u \mathcal{G}_{u} are strong π \pi -GC. So it suffices to show strong π \pi -GC for 𝒢 l \mathcal{G}_{l} and 𝒢 u \mathcal{G}_{u} . In the following analysis, we only show π \pi -GC for 𝒢 u \mathcal{G}_{u} , because the 𝒢 l \mathcal{G}_{l} case can be shown by the same argument. In order to prove strong π \pi -GC for 𝒢 u \mathcal{G}_{u} , we first demonstrate that, without loss of generality, we can assume that the class ℋ \mathcal{H} has a upper bound for | 𝔼 π X ​ [ h ​ ( X ) ] | \lvert\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[h(X)]\rvert , say | 𝔼 π X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] | ≤ M \lvert\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[h(X)]\rvert\leq M (so that Lemma J.12 can be used to propagate the GC property from ℋ \mathcal{H} to 𝒢 u \mathcal{G}_{u} ). Since sup h ∈ ℋ | 𝔼 π ^ X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] − 𝔼 π X ​ [ h ⁡ ( X ) ] | ≤ B ​ ‖ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ \sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\pi}_{X}}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[h(X)]\rvert\leq B\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty} , we have ϕ 1 ​ ( n , ϵ , ℋ ) ≤ ℙ ⁡ ( B ​ ‖ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ &gt; ϵ ) \phi_{1}(n,\epsilon,\mathcal{H})\leq\mathbb{P}(B\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty}&gt;\epsilon) . We bound the sub-Gaussian norm of ‖ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ \|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty} . Let X ( j ) , j = 1 , … , d X^{(j)},j=1,\ldots,d be the j j -th component of the random vector X ∈ ℝ d X\in\mathbb{R}^{d} . Since X i ( j ) , i = 1 , … , n X^{(j)}_{i},i=1,\ldots,n are i.i.d., we have ‖ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i ( j ) − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ( j ) ] ‖ ψ 2 ≤ C n ​ ‖ X ( j ) − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ( j ) ] ‖ ψ 2 \|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X^{(j)}]\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\|X^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X^{(j)}]\|_{\psi_{2}} for some universal constant C C , by general Hoeffding’s inequality (e.g., Proposition 2.6.1 in Vershynin 2018 ). Now we can use the sub-Gaussian maximal inequality (e.g., Lemma 2.2.2 in Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996 ) to get ‖ ‖ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ ‖ ψ 2 \displaystyle\|\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty}\|_{\psi_{2}} ≤ \displaystyle\leq C ′ ​ log ⁡ d ​ max 1 ≤ j ≤ d ​ ‖ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n X i ( j ) − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ( j ) ] ‖ ψ 2 \displaystyle C^{\prime}\sqrt{\log d}\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X^{(j)}]\|_{\psi_{2}} for another universal constant ​ C ′ \displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for another universal constant }C^{\prime} ≤ \displaystyle\leq C ′ ​ C ​ log ⁡ d n ​ max 1 ≤ j ≤ d ​ ‖ X ( j ) − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ( j ) ] ‖ ψ 2 \displaystyle C^{\prime}C\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\|X^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X^{(j)}]\|_{\psi_{2}} ≤ \displaystyle\leq C ′ ​ C ​ log ⁡ d n ​ ‖ ‖ X − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ ‖ ψ 2 \displaystyle C^{\prime}C\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}\|\|X-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty}\|_{\psi_{2}} where the last inequality holds because | X ( j ) − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ( j ) ] | ≤ ‖ X − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ \lvert X^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X^{(j)}]\rvert\leq\|X-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty} implies ‖ X ( j ) − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ( j ) ] ‖ ψ 2 ≤ ‖ ‖ X − 𝔼 π X ​ [ X ] ‖ ∞ ‖ ψ 2 \|X^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X^{(j)}]\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\|\|X-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{X}}[X]\|_{\infty}\|_{\psi_{2}} for all j = 1 , … , d j=1,\ldots,d .
